The Orlov spectra of Abelian categories ∗ Thanks: ∗ * 2010 Mathematics Subject Classification : 18G20, 16E10, 16G10. Thanks: Key words : Extension dimensions, Artin algebras, Orlov spectrums. Thanks: 1 1 Department of Mathematics, China Jiliang University, Hangzhou, 310018, Zhejiang Province, P. R. China. E-mail: zhengjunling@cjlu.edu.cn / zjlshuxue@163.com. Thanks: 2 2 School of Mathematics and statistics, Guizhou University, Guiyang 550025, Guizhou Province, P. R. China. E-mail: liuyz@gzu.edu.cn / yzliu3@163.com Thanks: † {\dagger} Corresponding author.
Junling Zheng 1, Yu-Zhe Liu 2,†

Abstract
. We introduced the notion of Orlov spectra of Abelian categories, and study its some properties. In particular, we give precise result of Orlov spectra of algebras with type 𝔸 n \mathbb{A}_{n} .



1.
Introduction

Rouquier introduced in
[
22
,
23
]
the dimension of a triangulated category under the idea of Bondal and van den Bergh in
[
8
]
.
Roughly speaking, it is an invariant that measures how quickly the category can be built from one object. This dimension plays an important role in representation theory

[
5
,
7
,
10
,
12
,
20
,
22
,
23
,
27
]
.
For example, it can be used to compute the representation dimension of Artin algebras
[
22
,
19
]
.
Similar to the dimension of triangulated categories, the (extension) dimension of an Abelian category was introduced by Beligiannis in
[
6
]
, also see
[
11
]
. The size of the extension dimension reflects how far an Artin algebra is from a finite representation type, some relate result can be seen in
[
26
]
.

The spectrum of a triangulated category is an invariant of the category introduced by Orlov in 2009 (see
[
21
]
). It can be seen as a generalization of the Rouquier dimension for triangulated categories. In 2012, Ballard, Favero, and Katzarkov systematically studied this invariant and named it the Orlov spectrum (see
[
5
]
). Similar to the Orlov spectrum of triangulated categories, we will define the Orlov spectrum for Abelian categories, which can be regarded as a generalization of the extension dimension for Abelian categories.
In this paper, we will mainly investigate some properties of the Orlov spectrum of finite representation type algebras.
We used the properties of coghost to demonstrate our main results, where coghost is a dual of ghost which is introduced by Lank in
[
17
]
.
The main theorems of our paper are as follows:

Theorem 1.1
.

(1)

(see Theorem
4.5
)

Let
0\longrightarrow L\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow N\longrightarrow 0

be an exact sequence in
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
. Then

\max\{\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(L),\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(N)\}\leqslant\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(M)\leqslant\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(L)+\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(N),

where
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}
is
t_{\mathsf{S}}
-radical layer length
(
see Definition
4.2
)
.
In particular, if
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(L)=0
, then
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(N)=\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(M);

if
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(N)=0
, then
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(L)=\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(M)
.

(2)

(see Theorem
4.14
)

If
\mathit{\Lambda}
is representation-finite, then

\Big\{\Big\lceil\dfrac{\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(\mathit{\Lambda})}{d}\Big\rceil-1\ \Big|\ d\in\mathbb{N}\text{ and }1\leqslant d<\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(\mathit{\Lambda})\Big\}\subseteq{\rm OSpec}(\mathit{\Lambda}),

where
\lceil\alpha\rceil
is the least integer greater than
\alpha
.

Furthermore, we obtain the following result by using Theorem
1.1
, cf. Propositions
5.1
and
5.4
.

Theorem 1.2

(Theorem
5.7
)
.

Let
\overrightarrow{\mathbb{A}}_{n}
be the linearly oriented quiver with type
\mathbb{A}_{n}
, i.e.,

\overrightarrow{\mathbb{A}}_{n}=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{n}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Then the Orlov spectrum
{\rm OSpec}(A_{n})
of
A_{n}:=\mathbf{k}\overrightarrow{\mathbb{A}}_{n}
is
\{0,1,2,\cdots,n-1\}.

2.
Preliminaries

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an Abelian category. In this paper, we assume that
all subcategories of
\mathcal{A}
are full, additive and closed under isomorphisms and all functors between categories are additive.
For a subclass
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{A}
, we use
\mathop{\rm add}\nolimits\mathcal{U}
to
denote the subcategory of
\mathcal{A}
consisting of
direct summands of finite direct sums of objects in
\mathcal{U}
,

and use
\langle\mathcal{U}\rangle
to denote the subcategory of
\mathcal{A}
containing all objects lying in
\mathcal{U}
.
For two objects
X
and
Y
in
\mathcal{A}
, we denote by
X\leq Y
if
X
is a subobject of
Y
, and denote by
X\leq_{\oplus}Y
if
X
is a direct summand of
Y
.
Let
\mathbb{N}
be the set of natural numbers and
\mathbb{N}^{+}=\mathbb{N}\backslash\{0\}
.

2.1.
The extension dimension of Abelian category

Let
\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{U}_{2},\cdots,\mathcal{U}_{n}
be subcategories of
\mathcal{A}
. Define

For a subcategory
\mathcal{U}

\mathcal{A}
, set

n\geqslant 2
,
and
[\mathcal{U}]_{\infty}=\mathop{\bigcup}_{n\geqslant 0}[\mathcal{U}]_{n}
, cf.
[
6
]
.

Example 2.1
.

Notice that
[\mathcal{U}]_{n}
and
[\mathcal{U}]_{n-1}\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ [\mathcal{U}]_{n-1}
are generally not equal.
To avoid this misunderstanding, we provide this example by using

A_{4}=\mathbf{k}\mathcal{Q}
with
\mathcal{Q}=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{2\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 64.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 64.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{3\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 99.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 99.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{4}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
.

Take
S=S(1)\oplus S(2)\oplus S(3)\oplus S(4)
be the direct sum of simple
A_{4}
-modules
S(1)
,
S(2)
,
S(3)
, and
S(4)
, and define

.
Then
[\mathcal{U}]_{2}=[\mathcal{U}]_{1}\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

consist of all indecomposable
A_{4}
-modules with dimensions
\leqslant 3
, that is,

[\mathcal{U}]_{2}=\{S(1),S(2),S(3),S(4),\big({{}_{4}^{3}}\big),\big({{}_{3}^{2}}\big),\big({{}_{2}^{1}}\big)\}.

For

Indeed, by the definition of

and

, we have

\displaystyle[\mathcal{U}]_{3}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ [\mathcal{U}]_{2}=[\mathcal{U}]_{2}\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ [\mathcal{U}]_{1}

\displaystyle=\Big\{S(1),S(2),S(3),S(4),\big({{}_{4}^{3}}\big),\big({{}_{3}^{2}}\big),\big({{}_{2}^{1}}\big),\Big(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
2\\
3\end{smallmatrix}\Big),\Big(\begin{smallmatrix}2\\

\displaystyle(\neq[\mathcal{U}]_{2}\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ [\mathcal{U}]_{2})

whose elements are the objects to be obtained by the extensions of
a module lying in
[\mathcal{U}]_{1}
and a module lying in
[\mathcal{U}]_{2}
.
We have
P(1)\notin[\mathcal{U}]_{3}
. Similarly, one can check that

[\mathcal{U}]_{4}=[\mathcal{U}]_{1}\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ [\mathcal{U}]_{3}=[\mathcal{U}]_{2}\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ [\mathcal{U}]_{1}=\mathsf{mod}A_{4}

because
P(1)\in[\mathcal{U}]_{4}
.

The following two lemmas are useful for this paper.

Lemma 2.2

(​
[
26
, Lemma 2.3]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an Abelian category and

T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathcal{A}
and
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then

(1)

[T_{1}]_{m}\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ [T_{2}]_{n}\subseteq[T_{1}\oplus T_{2}]_{m+n};

(2)

[T_{1}]_{m}\oplus[T_{2}]_{n}\subseteq[T_{1}\oplus T_{2}]_{\max\{m,n\}}.

Lemma 2.3

(​
[
26
, Lemma 2.4]
)
.

Let
F:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be an exact functor of Abelian categories.
Then
F([T]_{n})\subseteq[F(T)]_{n}
for any
T\in\mathcal{A}
and positive integer
n\geqslant 1.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
X
be an object in
\mathcal{A}
. The generation time of
X
,
denoted
\text{\ClockLogo}_{\mathcal{A}}(X)
is

\text{\ClockLogo}_{\mathcal{A}}(X):=\begin{cases}\inf\{n\in\mathbb{N}\mid\mathcal{A}=[X]_{n+1}\},&\text{if }[X]_{t}=\mathcal{A}\text{ for some }t\in\mathbb{N};\\
+\infty,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

The object
X
is called a
strong generator
of
\mathcal{A}
if
\text{\ClockLogo}_{\mathcal{A}}(X)
is finite.

Example 2.5
.

Consider the algebra
A_{4}
given in Example
2.1
and keep the notations from this example.
Then we have
\text{\ClockLogo}_{\mathsf{mod}A_{4}}(S)=4-1=3<+\infty
. Thus,
S
is a strong generator of
\mathsf{mod}A_{4}
.

Lemma 2.6
.

For an object
X
in
\mathcal{A}
with
\text{\ClockLogo}_{\mathcal{A}}(X)<+\infty
, we have

\text{\ClockLogo}_{\mathcal{A}}(X)\geqslant\text{\ClockLogo}_{\mathcal{A}}(X\oplus Y)

holds for arbitrary object
Y
in
\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

By
\mathop{\rm add}\nolimits X=[X]_{1}\subseteq[X\oplus Y]_{1}=\mathop{\rm add}\nolimits(X\oplus Y)
and induction, we have

(2.1)

\displaystyle[X]_{n+1}\subseteq[X\oplus Y]_{n+1}

since, for any integer
m\geqslant 0
,
[X]_{m}\subseteq[X\oplus Y]_{m}
yields

\displaystyle[X]_{m+1}=[X]_{1}\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ [X]_{m}\subseteq[X\oplus Y]_{1}\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ [X\oplus Y]_{m}=[X\oplus Y]_{m+1}.

Furthermore, (
2.1
) follows

\text{\ClockLogo}_{\mathcal{A}}(X)=\inf\{n\in\mathbb{N}\mid[X]_{n+1}=\mathcal{A}\}\geqslant\inf\{n\in\mathbb{N}\mid[X\oplus Y]_{n+1}=\mathcal{A}\}=\text{\ClockLogo}_{\mathcal{A}}(X\oplus Y)

by Definition
2.4
in the case for
\text{\ClockLogo}_{\mathcal{A}}(X)<+\infty
.
∎

Definition 2.7
.

The
Orlov spectrum

{\rm OSpec}(\mathcal{A})
of an Abelian category
\mathcal{A}
is defined as

{\rm OSpec}(\mathcal{A})=\{\text{\ClockLogo}_{\mathcal{A}}(X)\;|\;X\text{ is a strong generator of $\mathcal{A}$}\}.

The
extension dimension
(​​
[
6
]
) of
\mathcal{A}
is defined as the infimum of
{\rm OSpec}(\mathcal{A})
,
that is,

\mathop{\rm ext.dim}\nolimits\mathcal{A}:=\inf{\rm OSpec}(\mathcal{A}).

Dually, we can define the
ultimate dimension
of
\mathcal{A}
is defined as the supremum of
{\rm OSpec}(\mathcal{A})
,
that is,

{\rm u.dim}\mathcal{A}:=\sup{\rm OSpec}(\mathcal{A}).

For Artin algebra
\mathit{\Lambda}
, we set

{\rm OSpec}(\mathit{\Lambda}):={\rm OSpec}(\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda})
, and
{\rm u.dim}(\mathit{\Lambda}):={\rm u.dim}(\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda})
.

Lemma 2.8
.

If
[T]_{n+1}=\mathcal{A}\neq[T]_{n}
, then
n\in{\rm OSpec}(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof.

Since
[T]_{1}\subseteq[T]_{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq[T]_{n}\neq\mathcal{A}

and
[T]_{n+1}=\mathcal{A}
, we have
\text{\ClockLogo}_{\mathcal{A}}(T)=n
.
It follows
n\in{\rm OSpec}(\mathcal{A})
by the definition of Orlov spectrum (see Definition
2.7
).
∎

Example 2.9
.

Consider the algebra
A_{4}
given in Example
2.1
and keep the notations form this example.
By Example
2.5
, we have
3\in{\rm OSpec}(A_{4})
.
Moreover, by Lemma
2.8
, we can also prove
3\in{\rm OSpec}(A_{4})

since
[S]_{4}=\mathsf{mod}A_{4}\neq[S]_{3}
holds.

2.2.
Ghosts and coghosts

This section contains two parts as follows: in the first part we recall contravariantly finite subcategories and covariantly finite subcategories of Abelian categories;
and in the second part we recall ghosts introduced by Beligiannis (see
[
6
]
),

and coghosts introduced by Lank (see
[
17
, Definition 4.5]
)
.

The properties of coghosts are similar to that of ghosts.
However, for the convenience of reading, we still provide detailed proofs for properties of coghosts in our paper.

2.2.1.
Contravariantly finite subcategories and covariantly finite subcategories

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an Abelian category and
\mathcal{C}
be a subcategory of
\mathcal{A}
in this section.
A map
f:C\to X
in
\mathcal{A}
with
C\in\mathcal{C}

is called
a right
\mathcal{C}
-approximation
of
X
if for every

f^{\prime}:C^{\prime}\to X
with
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
there exists a map
g:C^{\prime}\to C

such that the following diagram

\textstyle{C^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X}

commutes, that is,
f^{\prime}=f\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2.4pt{\vbox to2.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.2pt\lower-1.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ g
.
If for every
X\in\mathcal{A}
admits a right
\mathcal{C}
-approximation, then
\mathcal{C}
is called
contravariantly finite
.
Dually, a map
f:X\to C
in
\mathcal{A}
with
C\in\mathcal{C}

is called a
left
\mathcal{C}
-approximation
of
X
if for every

f^{\prime}:X\to C^{\prime}
with
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
there exists a map
g:C\to C^{\prime}

such that the following diagram

\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{C^{\prime}}

commutes, that is,

. If for every
X\in\mathcal{A}
admits a left
\mathcal{C}
-approximation, then
\mathcal{C}
is called
covariantly finite
.

2.2.2.
Ghost lemma and Coghost lemma

Let
T
be an object in an Abelian category
\mathcal{A}
.

Definition 2.10

(​​
[
6
, Definition 1.2]
,
[
17
, Definition 4.5]
)
.

A morphism
f:Y\to A
(resp.
f:A\to Y
) in
\mathcal{A}
is called a
T
-ghost
(resp.
T
-coghost
) if the induced map
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}(T^{\prime},f)
(resp.
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}(f,T^{\prime})
) is zero for each
T^{\prime}\in\mathop{\rm add}\nolimits T
.
Furthermore, if a map can be written a composition of
n

T
-coghost maps, then it is called an
n
-fold ghost
(resp.
n
-fold coghost
).

Notation 2.11
.

For a set
S
of some objects lying in an Abelian category
\mathcal{A}
, we use
\langle S\rangle
be the minimal full subcategory containing
S
.
For any
n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, we set

(1)

\mathsf{Gh}^{n}_{T}(X,Y):=\{f\in\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}(X,Y)\mid f\text{ is an }n\text{-fold }T\text{-coghost map}\}
,

(2)

\mathsf{Gh}^{n}_{T}(-,Y):=\big\langle\mathsf{Gh}^{n}_{T}(X,Y)\mid X\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{A})\big\rangle
,

(3)

and
\mathsf{Gh}^{n}_{T}(X,-):=\big\langle\mathsf{Gh}^{n}_{T}(X,Y)\mid Y\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{A})\big\rangle
.

Remark 2.12
.

It is easy to see that
\mathsf{Gh}^{n}_{T}(X,Y)
is an ideals of
\mathcal{A}
, that is,
for any
T
-coghost
f:X\to Y
and arbitrary homomorphism
g:Y\to Z
(resp.,
g:Z\to X
),
the composition
g\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2.4pt{\vbox to2.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.2pt\lower-1.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(resp.,

) is also a
T
-coghost.

Recall that the

It follows that
\eta:\mathop{\rm Ker}\nolimits f\to X
is a
\mathcal{C}
-coghost by the definition of coghost.
∎

Let
\mathsf{Fac}(T)
(resp.
\mathsf{Sub}(T)
) be the subcategory of
\mathcal{A}
whose object is isomorphic to a quotient (resp. subobject) of some direct sum
T^{\oplus I}
, where
I
is a finite index set.
The following lemma, i.e., Ghost Lemma, is established by Beligiannis in
[
6
]
.

Lemma 2.14

(Ghost Lemma
[
6
, Lemma 1.3]
)
.

(a)

If
X\in[\mathsf{Fac}(T)]_{n}
, then

\mathsf{Gh}^{n}_{T}(X,-)=0
.

(b)

If
\mathop{\rm add}\nolimits T
is contravariantly finite in
\mathcal{A}
, then the following
are equivalent
:

(1)

\mathsf{Gh}^{n}_{T}(X,-)=0;

(2)

X\in[\mathsf{Fac}(T)]_{n}.

Next, we provide a dual result, say Coghost Lemma, for Ghost Lemma
which is first shown by Lank in
[
17
, Lemma 4.6]
.

Lemma 2.15

(Coghost Lemma)
.

(a)

If
Y\in[\mathsf{Sub}(T)]_{n}
, then

\mathsf{Gh}^{n}_{T}(-,Y)=0
.

(b)

If
\mathop{\rm add}\nolimits T
is covariantly finite in
\mathcal{A}
, then the following
are equivalent
:

(1)

\mathsf{Gh}^{n}_{T}(-,Y)=0;

(2)

Y\in[\mathsf{Sub}(T)]_{n}.

For the convenience of readers, we still provide a proof for Coghost Lemma.

Proof.

(a) If
n=1
. Let
f:X\to Y
be a
T
-coghost.
Since
Y\in[\mathsf{Sub}(T)]_{1}
, there is a monomorphism
g:Y\to T^{\prime}
such that
T^{\prime}\in\mathop{\rm add}\nolimits T
.
And, by the definition of coghost and
T^{\prime}\in\mathop{\rm add}\nolimits T
,
we have
g\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2.4pt{\vbox to2.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.2pt\lower-1.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ f=0
since
f:X\to Y
is a
T
-coghost.
Thus,
f=0
by using
g
to be a monomorphism.

,

.

\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits g_{i}\in[\mathsf{Sub}(T_{i})]_{1}\subseteq[\mathsf{Sub}(T)]_{1}
holds for each
0\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
, and then we have

\displaystyle=[\mathsf{Sub}(T)]_{n}.

Therefore, (1) implies (2) by induction.
∎

Remark 2.16
.

If
\mathcal{U}
is a subcategory of
\mathcal{V}
,
then
[\mathcal{U}]_{n}\subseteq[\mathcal{V}]_{n}
holds for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
see
[
26
, Proposition 2.2(2)]
.
Immediately, for each
T\in\mathcal{A}
, we have

[T]_{n}\subseteq[\mathsf{Fac}(T)]_{n}
and
[T]_{n}\subseteq[\mathsf{Sub}(T)]_{n}
.

2.3.
Coghosts on Artin algebra

Let
\mathit{\Lambda}
be an Artin
R
-algebra defined over Artin ring
R
in this section.
Then the category
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
of finitely generated right
\mathit{\Lambda}
-module (say
\mathit{\Lambda}
-module for short) is an Abelian category.
Now, we provide some properties of coghosts and strong generator in
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
which are dual of the properties of ghost.

2.3.1.
Some properties of coghost on Artin algebra

Let
T
be a finitely generated
\mathit{\Lambda}
-module. The following Lemma is well-known.

Lemma 2.17
.

The subcategory
\mathop{\rm add}\nolimits T
of
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
is a covariantly finite subcategory.

We will prove Proposition
2.18
through the above lemma.
Thus, for the convenience of readers, we still provide proof of the above lemma.

Proof.

First of all, for each
M\in\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
,
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathit{\Lambda}}(M,T)
can be seen as a finitely generated
R
-module defined by

\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathit{\Lambda}}(M,T)\times R\to\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathit{\Lambda}}(M,T),\ (h,r)\mapsto h\cdot r,

where
h\cdot r
is the map
M\to T
sending each
m\in M
to
f(m)r
,
see
[
4
]
.
Then
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathit{\Lambda}}(M,T)
containing a family of right
R
-homomorphism
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
which form a set of generators for
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathit{\Lambda}}(M,T)
.

Next, take an arbitrary map
g:M\to N
with
N\in\mathop{\rm add}\nolimits T
.
Notice that there is a positive integer
m
such that
N
is isomorphic to a direct summand of
T^{\oplus m}
(written as
N\leq_{\oplus}T^{\oplus m}
for simplification),
we can find two
\mathit{\Lambda}
-homomorphisms

\eta=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\eta_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\eta_{m}\end{smallmatrix}\right):N\to T^{\oplus m}

and

\theta=\left(\theta_{1}\ \cdots\ \theta_{m}\right):T^{\oplus m}\to N

such that
\theta\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2.4pt{\vbox to2.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.2pt\lower-1.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ \eta=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\theta_{i}\eta_{i}=1_{N}
.
Here,
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{m}
are
R
-homomorphisms in
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathit{\Lambda}}(N,T)
,
and
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{m}
are
R
-homomorphisms in
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathit{\Lambda}}(T,N)
.
Thus, for the
\mathit{\Lambda}
-homomorphism
\eta_{i}\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2.4pt{\vbox to2.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.2pt\lower-1.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ g\in\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathit{\Lambda}}(M,T)
,
there are
n
elements
b_{1j},\ldots,b_{nj}
such that

,
we have
Y_{0}\notin[T]_{n}.

Proof.

Notice that the composition
f_{1}\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2.4pt{\vbox to2.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.2pt\lower-1.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ f_{2}\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2.4pt{\vbox to2.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.2pt\lower-1.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ \cdots\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2.4pt{\vbox to2.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.2pt\lower-1.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ f_{n}\in\mathsf{Gh}^{n}_{T}(Y_{n},Y_{0})
is a non-zero
\mathit{\Lambda}
-homomorphism, we obtain
\mathsf{Gh}^{n}_{T}(Y_{n},Y_{0})\neq 0
.
Then, by Lemma
2.17
and Lemma
2.15
(b),
Y_{0}\notin[\mathsf{Sub}T]_{n}
holds.
Thus,
Y_{0}\notin[T]_{n}
since
[T]_{n}\subseteq[\mathsf{Sub}T]_{n}
by Lemma
2.16
.
∎

2.3.2.
Some properties of strong generator over Artin algebra

In this subdivision, we provide some properties for strong generator in the category
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
of finitely generated
\mathit{\Lambda}
-module over an Artin algebra
\mathit{\Lambda}
.

Proposition 2.19
.

If
T
is a strong generator of
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
, then any automorphism
f:X\to X
defined on non-zero
\mathit{\Lambda}
-module
X
is not a
T
-coghost.

Proof.

Assume
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}=[T]_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
.
If
f
is both an isomorphism and a
T
-coghost, then
f^{n}
is an isomorphism which is not zero.
In thise caes,
X\notin[T]_{n}=\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
by Property
2.18
, a contradiction.
∎

Proposition 2.20
.

Given a map
f:M_{1}\to M_{2}
in
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
.
If for each indecomposable module
N\in\mathop{\rm add}\nolimits T
,
either
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathit{\Lambda}}(M_{1},N)=0
or
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathit{\Lambda}}(M_{2},N)=0
,
then
f
is a
T
-coghost.

be an arbitrary module,
where all direct summands
N_{k}
of

are indecomposable.

for each

,
we have

Proposition 2.21
.

Given a map

in
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
.
If
f
is both
T_{1}
-coghost and
T_{2}
-coghost, then
f
is a
(T_{1}\oplus T_{2})
-coghost.

Proof.

For any morphism
g=\left({{}_{g_{2}}^{g_{1}}}\right)\in\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathit{\Lambda}}(M_{2},T_{1}^{\prime}\oplus T_{2}^{\prime})
, where

and

, we have

g\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2.4pt{\vbox to2.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.2pt\lower-1.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ f=\left({{}_{g_{2}}^{g_{1}}}\right)\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2.4pt{\vbox to2.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.2pt\lower-1.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

T_{2}
-coghost, we obtain
g_{1}\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2.4pt{\vbox to2.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.2pt\lower-1.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ f=0
and
g_{2}\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2.4pt{\vbox to2.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.2pt\lower-1.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

.
Then
g\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2.4pt{\vbox to2.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.2pt\lower-1.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ f=0
, i.e.,
f
is a
(T_{1}\oplus T_{2})
-coghost.
∎

The following lemma is well-known. We will use it to prove Proposition
2.23
.

Lemma 2.22
.

Let
M
and
N
be two
\mathit{\Lambda}
-modules in
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
.

(1)
If
N
is semi-simple, then

\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathit{\Lambda}}(N,M)=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathit{\Lambda}}(N,\mathop{\rm soc}\nolimits M).

(2)
If
M
is semi-simple, then

\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathit{\Lambda}}(N,M)=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathit{\Lambda}}(\mathop{\rm top}\nolimits N,M).

Proposition 2.23
.

If
T
is a strong generator, then
\mathop{\rm soc}\nolimits T\cong\mathop{\rm top}\nolimits T\cong\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda}
.

Proof.

We only show that
\mathop{\rm soc}\nolimits T\cong\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda}
, the proof of
\mathop{\rm top}\nolimits T\cong\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda}
is similar by using Ghost Lemma
2.14
.

First of all, we show that each simple
\mathit{\Lambda}
-modules is a direct summand of
\mathop{\rm soc}\nolimits T
.
Otherwise, there is a simple module
S\in\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
such that
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\Lambda}(S,T^{\prime})=0
holds for all
T^{\prime}\in\mathop{\rm add}\nolimits T
.
Then, clearly, any non-zero homomorphism
f:S\to S
is a
T
-coghost by Proposition
2.20
.
It contradicts with Proposition
2.19
because, by Schur’s lemma,
f
is an automorphism.
Thus,
S\leq_{\oplus}\mathop{\rm soc}\nolimits T
holds for each simple module
S
.
It follows that
\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda}\cong\bigoplus_{S}S\leq_{\oplus}\mathop{\rm soc}\nolimits T
as required.

On the other hand,
\mathop{\rm soc}\nolimits T\leq_{\oplus}\bigoplus_{S}S
is trivial.
Thus
\mathop{\rm soc}\nolimits T\cong\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda}
.
∎

Lemma 2.24
.

If
T
is a strong generator, then
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathit{\Lambda}}(Y,T)\neq 0
and
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathit{\Lambda}}(T,Y)\neq 0
hold for all
0\neq Y\in\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
.

Proof.

Since
f_{1}:Y\to\mathop{\rm top}\nolimits Y,\,\,f_{2}:\mathop{\rm top}\nolimits Y\to\mathop{\rm soc}\nolimits T=\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda}
, and
f_{3}:\mathop{\rm soc}\nolimits T\to T,

we have
f_{3}\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2.4pt{\vbox to2.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.2pt\lower-1.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ f_{2}\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2.4pt{\vbox to2.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.2pt\lower-1.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ f_{1}\neq 0
. Then
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\Lambda}(Y,T)\neq 0
.
∎

Proposition 2.25
.

If
T
is a strong generator of
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
,
then, for arbitrary projective or injection simple modules
S_{1},\ldots,S_{t}
,
the direct sum
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t}S_{i}
lies in
[T]_{1}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.24
, there is
\mathit{\Lambda}
-homomorphism
f:S\to T
for any simple module
S
such that
f\neq 0
. Thus,
f
is monomorphic by Schur’s lemma.
If
S
is projective, then
f
is a section, i.e.,

f\simeq\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1_{S}\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right):S\to S\oplus T^{\prime}\ (\cong T).

It follows that
S\leq_{\oplus}T
.
We can show that
S\leq_{\oplus}T
holds in the case for
S
to be injective by dual method.
Thus,
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t}S_{i}\in\mathop{\rm add}\nolimits T

=[T]_{1}
since
\mathop{\rm add}\nolimits T
is closed under direct sum.
∎

Proposition 2.26
.

T
is a strong generator in
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}

holds. Since

is not an isomorphism and

(1_{Y}-f\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2.4pt{\vbox to2.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.2pt\lower-1.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

3.
Loewy lengths

The
Loewy length

\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(M)
of a
\mathit{\Lambda}
-module
M
in
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
is defined as the length of the ascending chain

0=\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits^{n}M\leq\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits^{n-1}M\leq\cdots\leq\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits^{1}M\leq\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits^{0}M=M

where we use “
X\leq Y
” to represent a submodule (resp. subobject)
X
of
Y
in
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}
), and, for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
,
\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits^{i}M
is the submodule of
M
satisfying
\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits^{i}M=\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits^{i-1}M)
.
Obviously, the Loewy length of any semi-simple module is
1
, and the Loewy length of a
\mathit{\Lambda}
-module
M
is zero if and only if
M=0
.

3.1.
Properties of Loewy lengths

We recall some well-known results for Loewy length in this subsection.

Lemma 3.1
.

For arbitrary short exact sequence

0\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}X\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits^{f}{}Y\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits^{g}{}Z\to 0

in
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
, we have

\max\{\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(Y),\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(Z)\}\leqslant\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(Y)\leqslant\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(X)+\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(Z).

Lemma 3.2
.

For each
M\in[T]_{m}
, we have
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(M)\leqslant m\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(T)
.

Proof.

In the case of
m=1
, it is trivial that if
M\in[T]_{1}=\mathop{\rm add}\nolimits T
then
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(M)\leqslant\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(T)
.
Consider the induction hypothesis which admit that if
m\leq t
then
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(M)\leqslant t\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(T)
.
Then for
m=t+1
, there is a short exact sequence

0\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}A\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}M\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}B\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}0

such that
A\in[T]_{1}
and
B\in[T]_{t}
hold for arbitrary
M\in[T]_{t+1}
.
Then, by Lemma
3.1
, we have

\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(M)\leqslant\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(A)+\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(B)\leqslant 1\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(T)+t\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(T)=(1+t)\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(T).

Thus, this lemma holds by induction.
∎

Notice that if
T
is a strong generator of
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}

and the
m
is the positive integer satisfying
[T]_{m}=\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
,
then by Lemma
3.2
, the following two statement hold.

(1)

\ell\ell(\mathit{\Lambda})\leqslant m\ell\ell(T)
;

(2)

for each
M\in[\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda}]_{m}
, we have
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(M)\leqslant m\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda})=m
.

The statement (1) holds by
\mathit{\Lambda}\in[T]_{m}
; and the statement (2) can be shown in the case for
T=\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda}
.

Proposition 3.3
.

\text{\ClockLogo}_{\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}}(\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda})=\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})-1.

Proof.

The case
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})=1
is trivial. Now consider the
case
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})\geqslant 2.

For each
M\in\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda},

the following family of short exact sequence

(0\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits^{t}M\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits^{t-1}M\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits^{t-1}M/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits^{t}M\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}0)_{1\leqslant t\leqslant\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})-1}

satisfies
\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits^{t-1}M/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits^{t}M\in[\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda}]_{1}
for all
1\leqslant t\leqslant\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})-1

since all
\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits^{t-1}M/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits^{t}M
are semi-simple.
Then
[\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda}]_{\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})}=\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
.
It follows that
\text{\ClockLogo}_{\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}}(\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda})\leqslant\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})-1
.

On the other hand, if
\text{\ClockLogo}_{\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}}(\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda})\leqslant\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})-2
,
then
\mathit{\Lambda}\in\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}=[\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda}]_{\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})-1}
.
It follows
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})\leqslant(\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})-1)\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda})

by Lemma
3.2
, a contradiction.
∎

Corollary 3.4
.

The following two statements hold.

(1)

\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})-1\in{\rm OSpec}(\mathit{\Lambda}).

(2)

{\rm u.dim}(\mathit{\Lambda})\geqslant\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})-1.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.3
, we have

\text{\ClockLogo}_{\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}}(\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda})=\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})-1

holds for arbitrary Artin algebra
\mathit{\Lambda}
. Then the statement (1) holds by Definition
2.7
.
Furthermore, the statement (2) is obtained by (1) and the definition of ultimate dimension ( see Definition
2.7
) as following formula

{\rm u.dim}(\mathit{\Lambda})=\sup({\rm OSpec}(\mathit{\Lambda}))\geqslant\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})-1

as required.
∎

3.2.
Strong generators

Recall that the following well-known lemma which will be used to prove Lemma
3.6
in our paper.

Lemma 3.5
.

For any short exact sequence

0\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}A\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}B\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}C\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}0

in
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
, we have

\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits B\leqslant\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits(A\oplus C)
and
\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits B\leqslant\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits(A\oplus C)

1
1

1

For any module
X
in
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
, “
\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits X
” and “
\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits X
” are projective dimension and injective dimension of
X
, respectively.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
M
and
T
be two
\mathit{\Lambda}
-modules such that
M\in[T]_{n+1}
.
Then
\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits M\leqslant\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits T
and
\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits M\leqslant\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits T
.

Proof.

First of all, by
M\in[T]_{n+1}
, there is the following family of short exact sequences

(0\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}X_{i}\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}Y_{i-1}\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}Z_{i}\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}0)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}

such that
Z_{i-1}\leq_{\oplus}Y_{i-1}
(in this case,
Y_{i-1}\cong Z_{i-1}\oplus Z_{i-1}^{\prime}
for some
Z_{i-1}^{\prime}
),
where
Z_{0}=M
;
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in[T]_{1}
; and
Z_{i}\in[T]_{n-i+1}
.
Then, naturally, we have the following short exact sequences

0\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}\bigg(\bigoplus_{t=1}^{i}X_{t}\bigg)\oplus X_{i+1}\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}\bigg(\bigoplus_{t=1}^{i}X_{t}\bigg)\oplus Y_{i}\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}Z_{i+1}\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}0

for any
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
.
Then, by Lemma
3.5
and the above two families of short exact sequences, we have

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits M

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits Z_{0}\leqslant\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits Y_{0}\leqslant\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits(X_{1}\oplus Z_{1})

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits(X_{1}\oplus Z_{1}\oplus Z_{1}^{\prime})=\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits(X_{1}\oplus Y_{1})

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits(X_{1}\oplus X_{2}\oplus Z_{2})

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits(X_{1}\oplus X_{2}\oplus Z_{2}\oplus Z_{2}^{\prime})=\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits(X_{1}\oplus X_{2}\oplus Y_{2})

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits(X_{1}\oplus X_{2}\oplus X_{3}\oplus Z_{3})

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits(X_{1}\oplus X_{2}\oplus X_{3}\oplus Z_{3}\oplus Z_{3}^{\prime})=\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits(X_{1}\oplus X_{2}\oplus X_{3}\oplus Y_{3})

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\vdots

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits\bigg(\bigg(\bigoplus_{t=1}^{n}X_{t}\bigg)\oplus Y_{n}\bigg)\mathop{\leqslant}\limits^{\spadesuit}\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits T,

where
\spadesuit
holds since all
X_{i}
and
Y_{n}
are
\mathit{\Lambda}
-modules lying in
[T]_{1}=\mathop{\rm add}\nolimits T
.
We can prove
\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits M\leqslant\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits T
by similar way.
∎

Proposition 3.7
.

If
T
is a strong generator of
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
, then the global dimension
\mathop{\rm gl.dim}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda}
of
\mathit{\Lambda}
and the projective dimension
\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits T
of
T
coincide.

Proof.

Assume
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}=[T]_{n+1}
. Then we have
\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda})\leqslant\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits T

by Lemma
3.6
since it is clear that
\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda}
is a
\mathit{\Lambda}
-module in
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}=[T]_{n+1}
.
It follows that
\mathop{\rm gl.dim}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda}=\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda})\leqslant\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits T
.
On the other hand,
\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits T\leqslant\mathop{\rm gl.dim}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda}
by the definition of global dimension of the Artin algebra
\mathit{\Lambda}
.
Thus,
\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits T=\mathop{\rm gl.dim}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda}
as required.
∎

Corollary 3.8
.

Keep the notations from Proposition
3.7
, then the following two statements hold.

(1)

There is an indecomposable module
M
with

\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits M=\mathop{\rm gl.dim}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda}
such that
M\in[T]_{1}
.

(2)

There is an indecomposable module
N
with

\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits N=\mathop{\rm gl.dim}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda}
such that
N\in[T]_{1}
.

Proof.

We only prove (1), the proof of (2) is similar.
Considering the decomposition
T=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t}T_{i}
of
T
, where
T_{1},\ldots,T_{t}
are indecomposable,
we obtain

\mathop{\rm gl.dim}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda}=\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits T=\max_{1\leqslant i\leqslant t}\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits T_{i},

that is, there is an index
\imath
such that
\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits T=\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits T_{\imath}
as required.
∎

Next we provide an instance for Corollary
3.8
.

Example 3.9
.

Let
\mathit{\Lambda}=\mathbf{k}\mathcal{Q}/\mathcal{I}
be the basic algebra whose quiver and admissible ideal is given by

1\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits^{a}{}2\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits^{b}{}3\ \text{ and }\ \mathcal{I}=\langle ab\rangle,

respectively. One can check that
T:=S(1)\oplus S(2)\oplus S(3)
is a strong generator of the finitely generated module category
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
of
\mathit{\Lambda}
and the projective dimension of the direct summand
S(1)
of
T
equals to
2
.
Moreover, we have
\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits S(2)=1
and
\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits S(3)=0
.
Therefore,
\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits T=\mathop{\rm pd}\nolimits S(1)=\mathop{\rm gl.dim}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda}=2
in this example.

3.3.
Loewy lengths of some Artin alegbras

In this subsection we consider the Loewy lengths of some Artin algebras. This section containing three parts.
In the parts one, i.e.,
3.3.1
, we show that the Loewy length of an
SPI
Artin algebra
,
an Artin algebra whose simple modules are either projective or injective, is less than or equal to two.
In the parts two, i.e.,
3.3.2
, we provide a description of semi-simple algebra by using Loewy length.
In the last parts, i.e.,
3.3.3
, we prove that the Loewy length of an Artin algebra with Orlov spectrum
\{0,1\}
equals to two,
and compute the Orlov spectrum of any SPI Artin algebra.

3.3.1.
Loewy lengths of SPI Artin algebras

Proposition 3.10
.

If
\mathit{\Lambda}
is an
SPI
Artin algebra, then
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})\leqslant 2
.

Proof.

Assume that there is an SPI Artin algebra
\mathit{\Lambda}
such that all simple modules are either projective or injective and
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})=n\geqslant 3
.
Then there is a projective module
P
with
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(P)=n
.
In particular, we have

\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits P)=n-1
and
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits^{2}P)=n-2
.
The following short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits^{2}P\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits P\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits P/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits^{2}P\longrightarrow 0

admits
\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits P/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits^{2}P=Q\oplus E
such that
Q
is the sum of some simple projective modules and
E
is the sum of some simple injective modules by our assumption.
Then the pullback of

\textstyle{Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\leq_{\oplus}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}\text{canonical}\\
\text{epimorphism}\end{smallmatrix}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits P/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits^{2}P}

is of the following form.

(3.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 162.42722pt\raise-96.4189pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 169.40637pt\raise-120.61333pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 226.76404pt\raise-96.4189pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-127.55777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 42.88371pt\raise-127.55777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 94.90807pt\raise-127.55777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 163.90637pt\raise-127.55777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 226.76404pt\raise-127.55777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}.

Since
Q
is projective and
E
is injective, we have

W\cong\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits^{2}P\oplus Q
and
\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits P\cong W\oplus E
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle n-1

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits P)=\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(W\oplus E)=\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits^{2}P\oplus Q\oplus E)

\displaystyle=\max\{\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits^{2}P),\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(Q),\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(E)\}=n-2

by
n\geqslant 3
, a contradiction.
∎

Example 3.11
.

The inverse of Proposition
3.10
maybe not hold. For example, let the path algebra

\mathit{\Lambda}=\mathbf{k}\mathcal{Q}/\langle\alpha\beta,\beta\alpha\rangle
,
where the quiver
Q
as follows

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{2\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{\beta}

In this case, we have
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})=2
, but
S(1)
and
S(2)
are neither projective nor injective.

3.3.2.
Loewy lengths of semi-simple Artin algebras

Lemma 3.12
.

Assume that
\mathit{\Lambda}
is a representation-finite Artin algebra which is not semi-simple. Then
\{0,1\}\subseteq{\rm OSpec}(\mathit{\Lambda})
.

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{ind}(\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda})
be the set of all indecomposable
\mathit{\Lambda}
-modules (up to isomorphism).
Since
\mathit{\Lambda}
is representation-finite, that is,
\mathsf{ind}(\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda})
is a finite set, it is easy to see that

\displaystyle T=\bigoplus_{X\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda})}X

is a strong generator of
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
, and, in this case, we obtain
[T]_{1}=\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
.
Thus,
0\in{\rm OSpec}(\mathit{\Lambda})
.

On the other hand, there is an indecomposable projective module
P
whose Loewy length
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(P)
equals to the Loewy length
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})
of
\mathit{\Lambda}
. Let

T^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{X\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda})\backslash\{P\}}X,

then the following short exact sequence

0\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits P\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}P\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}P/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits P\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits{}0

shows that
P\in[T^{\prime}]_{2}
since
\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits P,P/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits P\in[T^{\prime}]
. On the other hand,

P\notin[T^{\prime}]_{1}.

That is,
[T^{\prime}]_{1}\subsetneq[T^{\prime}]_{2}=\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
.
It follows
1\in{\rm OSpec}(\mathit{\Lambda})
.
∎

Theorem 3.13
.

For an Artin algebra
\mathit{\Lambda}
, the following statement are equivalent.

(1)

\mathit{\Lambda}
is semi-simple.

(2)

\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})=1
.

(3)

{\rm OSpec}(\mathit{\Lambda})=\{0\}
.

Proof.

First of all, since
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})=1
, we obtain
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda})=0
.
It follows
\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda}=0
, i.e.,
\mathit{\Lambda}
is semi-simple. Thus, (2) admits (1).

Second, if
\mathit{\Lambda}
is semi-simple, then all indecomposable
\mathit{\Lambda}
-modules are simple modules which are direct summands of
\mathit{\Lambda}
.
It is easy to see that
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})=1
, Thus, (1) and (2) are equivalent.

Third, we show that (1) admits (3). Notice that semi-simple Artin algebra are always representation-finite.
Thus, we can assume that
\mathit{\Lambda}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}S_{i}
is basic up to Morita equivalence,
where
S_{1},\ldots S_{n}
are simple.
Then we obtain that each basic
\mathit{\Lambda}
-module
M
is isomorphic to

\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}S_{i}^{\oplus t_{i}},\ \ (t_{i}\in\{0,1\})

which lies in
[\mathit{\Lambda}]_{1}=\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
. Thus,
0\in{\rm OSpec}(\mathit{\Lambda})
.
However, if a
\mathit{\Lambda}
-module
T
does not contain some simple module
S_{i}
as its direct summand (up to isomorphism),
then
[T]_{n}\subsetneq\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
holds for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
{\rm OSpec}(\mathit{\Lambda})=\{0\}
.

Finally, it is easy to prove that
\mathit{\Lambda}
is semi-simple if
{\rm OSpec}(\mathit{\Lambda})=\{0\}
by Lemma
3.12
. Therefore, the statements (1), (2) and (3) are equivalent.
∎

3.3.3.
Loewy length of Artin algebras with Orlov spectra
\{0,1\}

The following result shows that the Loewy length of an Artin algebra with Orlov spectrum
\{0,1\}
equals to two.

Proposition 3.14
.

If
{\rm OSpec}(\mathit{\Lambda})=\{0,1\}
, then
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})=2
.

Proof.

If
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})=n\geqslant 3
, then
n\in{\rm OSpec}(\mathit{\Lambda})
by Proposition
3.3
(or Corollary
3.4
(1)), a contradiction. Thus,
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})\leqslant 2
.
By Theorem
3.13
and
{\rm OSpec}(\mathit{\Lambda})=\{0,1\}
, we obtain that
\mathit{\Lambda}
is not semi-simple, i.e.,
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})\geqslant 2
.
Thus,
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})=2
.
∎

Theorem 3.15
.

Let
\mathit{\Lambda}
be an
SPI
Artin algebra which is not semi-simple.

(1)

If
\mathit{\Lambda}
is representation-finite,
then
{\rm OSpec}(\mathit{\Lambda})=\{0,1\}.

(2)

If
\mathit{\Lambda}
is representation-infinite,
then
{\rm OSpec}(\mathit{\Lambda})=\{1\}.

Proof.

Assume
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}=[T]_{n+1}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. By Property
2.25
,
all simple modules lie in
[T]_{1}=\mathop{\rm add}\nolimits T
since every simple module is either a projective module or an injective module.
Thus,
T\cong(\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda})\oplus T^{\prime}
for some module
T^{\prime}
.
Then we obtain

\displaystyle\text{\ClockLogo}_{\text{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}}(T)

\displaystyle=\text{\ClockLogo}_{\text{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}}((\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda})\oplus T^{\prime})

(
by Lemma

2.6
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\text{\ClockLogo}_{\text{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}}(\mathit{\Lambda}/\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathit{\Lambda})

(
Proposition

3.3
)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})-1

(
Proposition

3.10
)

\displaystyle=2-1=1.

It follows that
{\rm u.dim}(\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda})=\sup({\rm OSpec}(\mathit{\Lambda}))\leqslant 1
.
On the other hand,

{\rm u.dim}(\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda})\geqslant\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda})-1=1
by Corollary
3.4
(2).
That is,
{\rm u.dim}(\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda})=1
. Thus,
1\in{\rm OSpec}(\mathit{\Lambda})
.

If
\mathit{\Lambda}
is representation-finite, then,
by Lemma
3.12
,
we have
{\rm OSpec}(\mathit{\Lambda})=\{0,1\}
, that is, the statement (1) holds;
otherwise,
{\rm OSpec}(\mathit{\Lambda})=\{1\}
, that is, the statement (2) holds.
∎

Next, we provide some examples for Theorem
3.15
.

Example 3.16
.

Consider the hereditary algebra
\mathit{\Lambda}=\vec{A}_{2}=\mathbf{k}\mathcal{Q}
with
\mathcal{Q}=1\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits^{\alpha}{}2
.
It is representation-finite, to be more precise, the number of isoclasses of indecomposable module equals to
3
.
Since the simple module
S(2)
is projective and the simple module
S(1)
is injective,
we have
{\rm OSpec}(\mathit{\Lambda})=\{0,1\}
by Theorem
3.15
(1).

Example 3.17
.

(1) Consider the path algebra
\mathit{\Lambda}=\mathbf{k}\mathcal{Q}
given by the following quiver shown in Figure
3.1
.

\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{2}
\alpha_{3}
\alpha_{4}
\alpha_{5}
\alpha_{n-1}

1

0

3

4

5

\cdots

\cdots

n

Figure 3.1.
The path algebra in Example
3.17
(1)

It is clearly that
\mathit{\Lambda}
is a hereditary algebra,
and then
\mathit{\Lambda}
is representation-infinite since
the underlying graph of
\mathcal{Q}
is not a Dynkin type
(cf.
[
1
, Chap VII, Section 2]
).
Moreover, the simple module
S(0)
is projective
and, for any
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, the simple module
S(i)
is injective.
Therefore,
{\rm OSpec}(\Lambda)=\{1\}
by Theorem
3.15
(2).

(2) Consider the path algebra
\Lambda=\mathbf{k}\mathcal{Q}
given by the quiver shown in Figure
3.2
, where
t
is an even number with
t\geqslant 4
.
Now we compute its Orlov spectrum.

First, it is clear that
\Lambda
is a hereditary algebra.
Then, it is not representation finite since
the underlying graph of
\mathcal{Q}
is not a Dynkin type
(cf.
[

, Chap VII, Section 2]
).
On the other hand, it is easy to see that the
simple modules
S(2i)
(

,
2\leqslant 2i\leqslant t
) is indecomposable projective
and
S(2j+1)
(
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
1\leqslant 2j+1\leqslant t
) is indecomposable injective.
Therefore,
{\rm OSpec}(\Lambda)=\{1\}
by Theorem
3.15
(2) as required.

4.
Radical Layer lengths

Layer length, introduced by Huard, Lanzilotta, and Hernández in
[
13
]
, is closely related to finitistic dimension.

4.1.
Torsion pairs and radical Layer lengths

Definition 4.1
.

A
torsion pair
(
torsion theory
) for
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
is a pair
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})

of classes of modules in
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
satisfying the following conditions:

(1)
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathit{\Lambda}}(M,N)=0
for each
M\in\mathcal{T}
and
N\in\mathcal{F}
;

(2) for each
X\in\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
, if
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathit{\Lambda}}(X,-)|_{\mathcal{F}}=0
then
X\in\mathcal{T}
;

(3) for each
Y\in\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
, if
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathit{\Lambda}}(-,Y)|_{\mathcal{T}}=0
then
Y\in\mathcal{F}
.

For a given class
\mathcal{X}
in
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
and a module
M\in\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
,
the trace of
\mathcal{X}
is the submodule
\text{Trace}_{\mathcal{X}}(M)
of

M
which is the sum of all images
\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits(f)
of the morphisms
f\in\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathit{\Lambda}}(X,M)
with
X\in\mathcal{X}.

Let
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})
be a torsion theory for
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
. Let us recall that
t:=\text{Trace}_{\mathcal{T}}

is the so called torsion radical attached to
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})
. In fact,
t(M)
is the largest submodule of
M

lying in
\mathcal{T}
. And we write

q_{t}:=1_{\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}}/t
, i.e.,
q_{t}(M):=M/t(M)
for each
M\in\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
.
Thus, for each
M\in\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
, we have the following short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow t(M)\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow q_{t}(M)\longrightarrow 0

where
t(M)\in\mathcal{T}
and
q_{t}(M)\in\mathcal{F}
.

Definition 4.2

(
[
13
]
)
.

Given a torsion pair
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})
with the torsion radical
t
.
The
t
-radical layer length
is a function

\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t}:\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}\to\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
via

\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t}(M)=\inf\{i\geqslant 0\mid t\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2.4pt{\vbox to2.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.2pt\lower-1.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ F_{t}^{i}(M)=0,M\in\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}\}

where
F_{t}=\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2.4pt{\vbox to2.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.2pt\lower-1.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ t
.

Let
\mathsf{S}
be a subset of some simple
\mathit{\Lambda}
-modules and
\mathsf{S}^{\prime}
the set of all the others simple
\mathit{\Lambda}
-modules.
A
\mathsf{S}
-filtration
of a
\mathit{\Lambda}
-module
M
is a filtration
0\subseteq M_{0}\subseteq M_{1}\cdots\subseteq M_{m-1}\subseteq M_{m}=M
such that
M_{i}/M_{i-1}\in\mathsf{S}
holds for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant m
.
We use
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{S}}
to represent the subcategory of
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
generated by all
\mathit{\Lambda}
-modules
M
with some
\mathsf{S}
-filtration.
We have the following statements:

•

it is easy to check that
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{S}}
is closed under extensions, submodules and quotients modules;

•

and there is a subcategory
\mathcal{T}_{\mathsf{S}}
of
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
such that
(\mathcal{T}_{\mathsf{S}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{S}})
is a torsion pair
(the corresponding torsion radical is denoted by
t_{\mathsf{S}}
),

and have the following results.

Proposition 4.3

(​​
[
27
, Proposition 3.2]
)
.

If
\mathsf{S}=\varnothing
, then
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(M)=\ell\ell(M)
for each
M\in\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let the pair
(\mathcal{T}_{\mathsf{S}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{S}})
be a torsion pair as above.

(1)

(​
[
15
, Proposition 5.9]
)

\mathcal{T}_{\mathsf{S}}=\{M\in\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}\mid\mathop{\rm top}\nolimits(M)\in\mathop{\rm add}\nolimits(\mathsf{S}^{\prime})\}
;

(2)

(​
[
15
, Lemma 3.3(c)]
)

If
M\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathsf{S}}
and
M\neq 0
, then
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(M)=\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits M)+1;

(3)

(​
[
15
, Lemma 3.4(a)]
)

\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}M_{i})=\sup_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(M_{i})
,
where
M_{i}\in\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
.

4.2.
Short exact sequences and radical layer length

In this section, we show two main results, i.e., Theorems
4.5
and
4.14
, of our paper.

4.2.1.
The first main result and some corollaries

In this subdivision, we show the following theorem which is the first result of our paper.

Theorem 4.5
.

Let
0\longrightarrow L\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow N\longrightarrow 0

be an exact sequence in
\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
. Then

\max\{\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(L),\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(N)\}\leqslant\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(M)\leqslant\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(L)+\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(N).

In particular, if
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(L)=0
, then
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(N)=\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(M);

if
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(N)=0
, then
\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(L)=\mathop{\rm\ell\ell}\nolimits^{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(M)
.

We need some lemmas for proving it.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
X
be a
\mathit{\Lambda}
-module.

(1)

(​
[
13
, Proposition 5.9 (c)]
)

t_{\mathsf{S}}(\mathit{\Lambda}_{\mathit{\Lambda}})
is a two side ideal of
\mathit{\Lambda}
and
t_{\mathsf{S}}(X)=X\cdot t_{\mathsf{S}}(\mathit{\Lambda}_{\mathit{\Lambda}})
.

(2)

(​
[
4
, Propostion 3.5]
)

\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits X=X\cdot\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda}_{\mathit{\Lambda}})
.

(3)

\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits(t_{\mathsf{S}}(\mathit{\Lambda}_{\mathit{\Lambda}}))=t_{\mathsf{S}}(\mathit{\Lambda}_{\mathit{\Lambda}})\cdot\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda}_{\mathit{\Lambda}})
.

(4)

t_{\mathsf{S}}(\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits(t_{\mathsf{S}}(\mathit{\Lambda}_{\mathit{\Lambda}})))=t_{\mathsf{S}}(\mathit{\Lambda}_{\mathit{\Lambda}})\cdot\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits(\mathit{\Lambda}_{\mathit{\Lambda}})\cdot t_{\mathsf{S}}(\mathit{\Lambda}_{\mathit{\Lambda}})
.

(5)

t_{\mathsf{S}}(F_{t_{\mathsf{S}}}^{i}(\mathit{\Lambda}_{\mathit{\Lambda}}))
is an ideal of
\mathit{\Lambda}
for each
i\geqslant 0
.

Proof.

The statement (3) can be obtained by (2) in the case of
X=t_{\mathsf{S}}(\mathit{\Lambda}_{\mathit{\Lambda}})
.
The statement (4) is obtained by (1) and (3), and the statement (5) is obtained by (1)–(4).
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

For any module
X\in\mathsf{mod}\mathit{\Lambda}
, we have

t_{\mathsf{S}}(F_{t_{\mathsf{S}}}^{i}(X))=X\cdot t_{\mathsf{S}}(F_{t_{\mathsf{S}}}^{i}(\Lambda_{\Lambda}))
for each
i\geqslant 0
.

4.6

. Assume that if

, we have

\displaystyle t_{\mathsf{S}}(F_{t_{\mathsf{S}}}^{n+1}(X))

\displaystyle=t_{\mathsf{S}}(F_{t_{\mathsf{S}}}^{n}(F_{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(X)))

(
using assumption
)

\displaystyle=F_{t_{\mathsf{S}}}(X)\cdot t_{\mathsf{S}}(F_{t_{\mathsf{S}}}^{n}(\mathit{\Lambda}_{\mathit{\Lambda}}))

(
F_{t_{\mathsf{S}}}=\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\ \lower-0.86108pt\hbox{\hbox to2.4pt{\vbox to2.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.2pt\lower-1.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(

)

(2)
)

\displaystyle=t_{\mathsf{S}}(X)\cdot\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits(t_{\mathsf{S}}(F_{t_{\mathsf{S}}}^{n}(\mathit{\Lambda}_{\mathit{\Lambda}})))

)
